{"472495/e03cd0b7ebe196cc5c373daa5981ddaddf572bdc": "Immediately stop trying to accept more connections if listener disabled\n\nThis is a refined version of the logic previously in #578\n\nThe rationale is that the consumer of sockets may wish to temporarily\ndelay accepting for some reason (e.g. being out of file-descriptors).\nThe kernel will then queue them up. The kernel queue is bounded and\nprograms like NodeJS will actually try to quickly accept and then close\n(as the current behaviour before this PR).\n\nHowever, it seems that libevent should allow the user to choose whether\nto accept and respond correctly if the listener is disabled."}
{"472495/0379eb4b5d0de2f1eac673d75c60358b85a220c0": "Do not ship evconfig-private.h in dist archive\n\nIt will be generated by autotools, so there is not reason to include it.\nAnd infact this breaks compilation with out-of-tree builds (VPATH),\nsince, for the quote form of the include directive, headers in the\ndirectory of the file with the #include line have priority over those\nnamed in -I options, the copy of evconfig-private.h from the source\ndirectory had priority over the one in the build directory."}
{"472495/1af8017648d63d461737805e2607174c38708950": "[core] re-order fields in struct event for memory efficiency\n\nThe sizeof `struct event` can reduced on both 32 bit and 64 bit systems\nby moving the 4 bytes that make up `ev_events` and `ev_res` below `ev_fd`,\nbefore `struct event_base * ev_base;` since our compiler wouldn't dare do\nsuch a thing (it instead will pad twice, whereas it only needs to be padded\nonce)\n\n```C\nstruct event {\n                                                         /*  OFFS |  SZ   Bytes  | Total Bytes | START - END  */\n    struct event_callback ev_evcallback;                 /*   0x0 |  40          | 40          | 0x0   - 0x28 */\n\n    union {                                              /*  0x28 |  ----------- | ----------- | ------------ */\n        TAILQ_ENTRY(event) ev_next_with_common_timeout;  /*       |       ((16)) |             |              */\n        int min_heap_idx;                                /*       |       ((04)) |             |              */\n    }   ev_timeout_pos;                                  /*       |  16          | 56          | 0x28  - 0x38 */\n    int ev_fd;                                           /*  0x38 |  04          | 60          | 0x38  - 0x3c */\n```\n\nSince the next field is 8 bytes in length, and we are up to 60 bytes, `ev_fd` ends up being padded (4 more bytes on 64b).\n\n```C\n                              /* --- 1 byte gap HERE --->            1           | <61>                      */\n                              /* --- 1 byte gap HERE --->            1           | <62>                      */\n                              /* --- 1 byte gap HERE --->            1           | <63>                      */\n                              /* --- 1 byte gap HERE --->            1           | <64>                      */\n    struct event_base * ev_base;                         /*  0x3c |  8           | 68         | 0x3c - 0x40  */\n    union {                                              /*  0x40 | ------------ | ---------- | ------------ */\n        struct {                                         /*       | ------------ |            |              */\n            LIST_ENTRY (event) ev_io_next;               /*       |       ((16+  |            |              */\n            struct timeval ev_timeout;                   /*       |         16)) |            |              */\n        } ev_io;                                         /*       | ((32))       |            |              */\n        struct {                                         /*       | ------------ |            |              */\n            LIST_ENTRY (event) ev_signal_next;           /*       |      ((16+   |            |              */\n            short   ev_ncalls;                           /*       |        02+   |            |              */\n            short * ev_pncalls;                          /*       |        08))  |            |              */\n        } ev_signal;                                     /*       | ((26))       |            |              */\n    } ev_;                                               /*  0x60 | 32           | 100        | 0x40 - 0x60  */\n\n    short ev_events;                                     /*  0x60 | 2            | 102        | 0x60 - 0x62  */\n    short ev_res;                                        /*  0x62 | 2            | 104        | 0x62 - 0x64  */\n```\n\nWe now hit another line, `struct timeval` is 16 bytes on 64b arch, so we have 4 more bytes\nof padding on `ev_res`.\n\n```C\n    /* --- 1 byte gap HERE --- */\n    /* --- 1 byte gap HERE --- */\n    /* --- 1 byte gap HERE --- */\n    /* --- 1 byte gap HERE --- */\n    struct timeval ev_timeout;                           /*  0x64 | 16           | 120        | 0x64 - 0x74  */\n};\n```\n\nAfter moving `ev_events` and `ev_res` below `ev_fd` we have something\na bit more optimal:\n\n```C\nstruct event2 {\n                                                         /*  OFFS |  SZ / Bytes  | RSUM Bytes | START - END  */\n    struct event_callback ev_evcallback;                 /*   0x0 | 40           | 40         | 0x0   - 0x28 */\n    union {                                              /*  0x28 | ------------ | ---------- | ------------ */\n        TAILQ_ENTRY(event) ev_next_with_common_timeout;  /*       |       ((16)) |            |              */\n        int min_heap_idx;                                /*       |       ((04)) |            |              */\n    } ev_timeout_pos;                                    /*       | 16           | 56         | 0x28  - 0x38 */\n    int ev_fd;                                           /*  0x38 | 4            | 60         | 0x38  - 0x3c */\n    short ev_events;                                     /*  0x3c | 2            | 62         | 0x3c  - 0x3e */\n    short ev_res;                                        /*  0x3e | 2            | 64         | 0x3e  - 0x40 */\n\n    struct event_base * ev_base;                         /*  0x40 | 8            | 74         | 0x40  - 0x48 */\n    union {                                              /*  0x48 | ------------ | ---------- | ------------ */\n        struct {                                         /*       | ------------ |            |              */\n            LIST_ENTRY (event) ev_io_next;               /*       |        ((16+ |            |              */\n            struct timeval ev_timeout;                   /*       |         16)) |            |              */\n        } ev_io;                                         /*       | ((32))       |            |              */\n        struct {                                         /*       | ------------ |            |              */\n            LIST_ENTRY (event) ev_signal_next;           /*       |       ((16+  |            |              */\n            short   ev_ncalls;                           /*       |         02+  |            |              */\n            short * ev_pncalls;                          /*       |         08)) |            |              */\n        } ev_signal;                                     /*       | ((26))       |            |              */\n    } ev_;                                               /*       | 32           | 106        | 0x48 - 0x68  */\n    struct timeval ev_timeout;                           /* 0x68  | 16           | 120        | 0x68 - 0x78  */\n};\n```\n\nWe still have a gap here, but the first was removed.\n\nAgain, we can save 8 bytes on both 32 and 64 word sizes (32/64 byte cacheline).\n\nBelow are the results for testing v2.1.6 -> master -> master + this patch (Release/-O3)\n\nCode:\n\n```C\n  #include <event2/event.h>\n\n  int\n  main(int argc, char ** argv) {\n      printf(\"%zu\\n\", event_get_struct_event_size());\n      return 0;\n  }\n```\n\nBranch: `master` (2.2.x)\n\n```\n  $ gcc -O3 -Wall -Wl,-R/usr/local/lib bleh.c -L/usr/local/lib -o bleh  -levent\n  $ ldd bleh\n      linux-vdso.so.1 =>  (0x00007ffc3df50000)\n      libevent.so.2.2.0 => /usr/local/lib/libevent.so.2.2.0 (0x00007f91fd781000)\n      libc.so.6 => /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6 (0x00007f91fd3a1000)\n      libpthread.so.0 => /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libpthread.so.0 (0x00007f91fd182000)\n      /lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2 (0x00007f91fdbcc000)\n  $ ./bleh\n  128\n```\n\nRelease: `2.1.6`\n\n```\n  $ gcc -O3 bleh.c -o bleh  -levent\n  $ ldd bleh\n      linux-vdso.so.1 =>  (0x00007ffd43773000)\n      libevent-2.1.so.6 => /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libevent-2.1.so.6 (0x00007feb3add6000)\n      libc.so.6 => /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6 (0x00007feb3a9f6000)\n      libpthread.so.0 => /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libpthread.so.0 (0x00007feb3a7d7000)\n      /lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2 (0x00007feb3b22a000)\n  $ ./bleh\n  128\n```\n\nBranch: `this one`\n\n```\n  $ gcc -O3 -Wl,-R./lib bleh.c -o bleh -L./lib -levent\n  $ ldd bleh\n      linux-vdso.so.1 =>  (0x00007ffff55f7000)\n      libevent.so.2.2.0 => ./lib/libevent.so.2.2.0 (0x00007ff8e5c82000)\n      libc.so.6 => /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6 (0x00007ff8e58a2000)\n      libpthread.so.0 => /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libpthread.so.0 (0x00007ff8e5683000)\n      /lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2 (0x00007ff8e60cd000)\n  $ ./bleh\n  120\n```"}

{"472495/b59525ece4288d844fa31945eedddd859d58bfaa": "evdns: fix race condition in evdns_getaddrinfo()\n\nevdns_getaddrinfo() starts two parallel requests for A and AAAA record.\nBut if request is created from thread different from dns_base's, request of A record is\nstarted immediately and may result in calling free_getaddrinfo_request() from\nevdns_getaddrinfo_gotresolve() because `other_req' doesn't exist yet.\n\nAfter that, request of AAAA record starts and finishes, and evdns_getaddrinfo_gotresolve()\nis called again for structure that is already freed.\n\nThis commits adds locking into evdns_getaddrinfo() function."}

{"73372814/ff031ab388e00afd5216cfb98505e4b59e9cfae2": "hashmap: v2.0 initial commit (new API + algorithm improvements)\n\nHashmap 2.0 Highlights:\n* New generic and type-safe API. We no longer need to use a macro to generate type-safe wrapper functions.\n* Improved linear probing algorithm. The previous algorithm could fail on insert, rehash, or remove if a particularly poor hash function was provided. The new algorithm can never fail, even with a worst-case hash function. This adds user confidence and reduces failure modes.\n* Added a supplemental hash function. Linear probing is especially sensitive to clustering due to poor hash functions. Since the hash function is user-supplied, adding a supplemental hash function provides more consistent performance.\n* Now, always provide hashmap statistics API with no additional overhead to ordinary hashmap operations.\n* Now, do lazy allocation on init. We reserve no memory on the heap until the first item is added.\n* Default hashmap size is reduced to 128 elements.\n* A hashmap_reserve() function was added to pre-allocate the hashmap.\n* hashmap_foreach macros have been added to hide the complexities of iterator usage and streamline iteration."}

GNU ld (version 2.17 on OpenBSD)\ndon't know `--build-id`, but they know `-b input-format` which patchelf\ntriggers by ommitting the second dash.\n\nLLD's ld(1) seems to cope with this, although it's incorrect usage.\n\nUse the double dash for correctness to get accurate error messages with\nsuch incompatible linkers:\n\n\t-/usr/bin/ld: invalid BFD target `uild-id'\n\t+/usr/bin/ld: unrecognized option '--build-id'\n\t+/usr/bin/ld: use the --help option for usage information"}

{"1139119/c2c94024f5d15c2fe36c72cb139df6a2ccd9b3ec": "build: Add symbol versions to all exported symbols\n\nWith this version script, newly-linked binaries that depend on the\njson-c shared library will refer to its symbols in a versioned form,\npreventing their references from being resolved to a symbol of the same\nname exported by json-glib or libjansson if those libraries appear in\ndependency search order before json-c, which will usually result in\na crash. This is necessary because ELF symbol resolution normally uses\na single flat namespace, not a tree like Windows symbol resolution.\nAt least one symbol (json_object_iter_next()) is exported by all three\nJSON libraries.\n\nLinking with -Bsymbolic is not enough to have this effect in all cases,\nbecause -Bsymbolic only affects symbol lookup within a shared object,\nfor example when json_object_set_serializer() calls\njson_object_set_userdata(). It does not affect calls from external\ncode into json-c, unless json-c was statically linked into the\nexternal caller.\n\nThis change will also not prevent code that depends on json-glib or\nlibjansson from finding json-c's symbols and crashing; to prevent\nthat, a corresponding change in json-glib or libjansson would be needed.\n\nAdding a symbol-version is a backwards-compatible change, but once\nadded, removing or changing the symbol-version on a symbol would be an\nincompatible change that requires a SONAME bump.\n\nResolves: https://github.com/json-c/json-c/issues/621\n(when combined with an equivalent change to libjansson).\n\nSigned-off-by: Simon McVittie <smcv@collabora.com>"}
{"1139119/1962ba7de30b999a9d08c716fe588b8e562101e3": "Fixes #645\nHello, I am a colleague of @jakub-zwolakowski. There is a last minor\nproblem we found by running extra tests that I thought you might want\nto hear about.\nIn json_tokener.c, the standard function tolower is used to\nrecognize the multiple forms that infinity can take:\ninf_char = tolower((int)*str);\n\nHere str is a pointer to char and is used to access a char\nstraight from the input. char is a signed type on most platforms,\nin particular x86.\nThe clause 7.4:1 of the C17 standard applies to tolower:\n\nIn all cases the argument is an int, the value of which shall be representable as an unsigned char\nor shall equal the value of the macro EOF. If the argument has any other value, the behavior is\nundefined.\n\nIn principle, a libc could implement tolower with a\nlook-up table and omit the bound checks on the argument. If JSON-C\nwere used with such a libc, a malicious input containing well-placed\nnegative chars could cause an out-of-bounds read, possibly leading to a crash.\nSince the behavior is undefined, an implementation of tolower could also\nreturn 'i', 'n', 'f', 'n', 't', or 'y' for some negative char\nvalues, leading the parser to recognize \u201cinfinity\u201d in inputs that do\nnot contain this word.\nIn practice, Glibc does implement tolower with a look-up table, but\nit validates the range of the input first, and musl-libc uses a\nstraightforward if to detect uppercase letters.  Neither of these\npopular implementations will have the hypothetical problems above.\nInterestingly, in the current version of the line, the char *str is\nexplicitly cast to int before being passed to tolower.  This cast\nwas introduce by this commit: Add cast to int on tolower() to fix\nwarnings about array subscripts.\nThe warning in question is likely GCC's warning about using a char\nexpression as array index, and we can infer that the headers of some\ncompilation platforms also expand tolower(e) to an expression in\nwhich e is used as an array index (hopefully after having been\nvalidated, but the compiler is not sophisticated enough to recognize\nthis and omit the warning).\nI would suggest the following line as a replacement:\ninf_char = tolower((unsigned char)*str);\n\nThis would silence GCC's  -Wchar-subscripts warning and it would\nalso ensure that the function tolower is used according to the\nminimal guarantees provided by the C standard.\n\nI suggest opening a quick pull request with this proposed change.\n"}

{"1139119/f63573460c8e077af75254ddfff6761404920235": "Fix typo"}
{"1139119/213bb5caa11ed2182848d86c86f8e9ab4c75642a": "Fix memory leak with emtpy strings in json_object_set_string\n\nWhen a json string object is updated with a bigger string, a new\nmalloc'ed buffer is used to store the new string and it's size is made\nnegative to indicate that an external buffer is in use.\n\nWhen that same json string object get's updated again with an empty\nstirng (size = 0), the new external malloc'ed buffer is still used.\nBut the fact that the new size value is not negative removes the\nindicator that the externally malloc'ed buffer is used.\n\nThis becomes a problem when the object get's updated again with any\nother string, because a new buffer will be malloced and linked to the\nobject while to old one won't be free'd.\n\nThis causes a memory leak when updating a json string with\njson_object_set_stirng() which has previously been updated\nwith an empty string.\n\nExample:\n--\nobj = json_object_new_string(\"data\");\njson_object_set_string(obj, \"more data\");\njson_object_set_string(obj, \"\");\njson_object_set_string(obj, \"other data\"); /* leaks */\n--\n\nThis commit fixes the issue by free'ing the external buffer when an\nempty string is set and use the internal one again.\n\nSigned-off-by: Daniel Danzberger <daniel@dd-wrt.com>"}

I know that most commercial UNIX's will blow up if you use it.\n\n\nsprintf() is void on some pre-ANSI platforms; I can add a test for this or use snprintf() to get a guaranteed return value instead of using slen, but that part of the part isn't portable.\n\n\nI like the changes to mxml_file_putc() and will incorporate them.\n\n\nThe changes in the main() function of mxmldoc (using %ste and fprintf instead of fputs) are unnecessary and actually make the code less efficient.\n\n\nThe changes in the add_variable() function of mxmldoc (manual string copy instead of strcpy and strlen calls) may or may not be more efficient - strlen and strcpy are heavily optimized on some platforms, which may make them faster than the manual code method. I will do some benchmarking to see if there is any perceptable difference.\n\n\nSimilarly, the changes to use your scan4word() function may or may not provide a measurable improvement; will try things out and see if there is any.\n\n\nThe big printf() calls replacing the multiple puts() calls should be OK, although since no format replacements are being done we can use puts() for the combined strings. I will verify that the strings aren't over 32k (should be, but that is a common limit on older platforms/compilers) and apply this change.\n\n\nThe mass conversion of all puts() calls to printf() calls will actually slow things down substantially - printf() has a lot more overhead than puts(). Given the limited potential savings in code space, I'd rather have mxmldoc run faster.\n\n\nprintf is slower by far than puts, fputs, fputc, and putc. It makes sense to replace putc, print, putc and similar sequences with a single printf, however I won't replace everything with a printf.\n\n\nI will post a proposed patch against 2.0rc1 that {"29804085/14dc4a7781a74a156a418690467554bae4a79b97": "Abort on strbuf errors\n\nUsers of the strbuf API are supposed to check for an OOM condition\nafter appending to strbufs, but:\n\n* This is never done in the whole code base.\n* The implementation was flawed because only `ptr` was set to the\n  OOM value without adjusting `size` and `asize`. After an error,\n  subsequent calls could very well lead to segfaults, contrary to the\n  documentation.\n\nChange the code to always abort on errors with a message printed to\nstderr. The only alternative is to propagate errors throughout the\nwhole library which seems infeasible."}

{"19330466/eec5700f4d62113b47ee548edbc4746f61ffb098": "added some notes of constant selection\n\nas suggested in #151.\nWhile reading\nhttps://github.com/Cyan4973/xxHash/wiki/xxHash-specification-(draft)\nI noticed that the constants like static const u64 PRIME64_1 = 11400714785074694791ULL; are just \"magically\" given without justification. It would be good to know why those numbers are chosen the way they are, or whether the values don't matter and any randomly chosen odd number would work.\n\nSure,\ngenerally speaking, they feature a good mix of bits 1 and 0, neither too regular, nor too dissymmetric. They are also prime numbers, at least for the 32-bits ones. I don't remember exactly for 64-bits ones.\nBeing prime numbers is not strictly required (the only requirement is for these numbers to be odd), but it certainly doesn't hurt.\nAnd finally, they featured good behavior during tests, which is the most important criteria.\nI could add a few notes on this topic in the specification.\n\nThe specification has been updated in dev branch to provide a rationale for selection of constants : https://github.com/Cyan4973/xxHash/blob/dev/doc/xxhash_spec.md\n"}

{"14812739/b3ab332b340fb857c4d6bd43208aa708a6eb00bd": "unix: fix EMFILE error handling\n\nOn Linux, the accept() and accept4() system calls checks for EMFILE\nbefore checking for EAGAIN. Refine our EMFILE error handling tactic\nto deal with that. Here is what used to happen:\n\n 1. The event loop calls accept() and sees EMFILE.\n\n 2. Libuv switches to EMFILE error handling mode. It closes a stashed\n    file descriptor and calls accept() again.\n\n 3. The accept() system call fails with EAGAIN.\n\n 4. Libuv reopens the stashed file descriptor (reaching RLIMIT_NOFILE\n    again) and returns control to the regular event loop.\n\n 5. The regular event loop calls accept(), sees EMFILE and jumps to\n    step 2 again. Effectively an infinite loop.\n\nAvoid that by not calling accept() again when we've seen EAGAIN.\n\nFixes joyent/node#5389.\nNode enters an infinite loop (or something that makes it to take 100% CPU) when spawning enough long running processes that we hit the simultaneous open file limit. We can demonstrate this with following application:\nvar child_process = require(\"child_process\");\nvar http = require('http');\n\nfunction requestCallback(req, res) {\n  var sleep = null;\n  try {\n    console.log(\"Spawning\");\n    sleep = child_process.spawn(\"sleep\", [\"60\"]);\n    console.log(\"Spawned\");\n  } catch (error) {\n    console.log(\"Error: \" + error);\n    res.writeHead(500, {'Content-Type': 'text/plain'});\n    res.end('Internal server error\\n');\n    return;\n  }\n  sleep.stdout.on('data', function (data) {});\n  sleep.stderr.on('data', function (data) {});\n  sleep.on(\"error\", function() {\n      throw new Error(\"No sleep executable 'sleep' in path!\");\n  });\n  sleep.on('exit', function (code) {\n    console.log(\"Hello!\");\n    res.writeHead(200, {'Content-Type': 'text/plain'});\n    res.end('Hello World\\n');\n  });\n}\n\nserver = http.createServer(requestCallback);\nserver.listen(8080, \"0.0.0.0\");\nThen open and close a lot of concurrent connections in such way that the process remains running at the background. For example by using Apache bench:\n$ ab -c 500 -n 1500 http://server:8080/\nThis is ApacheBench, Version 2.3 <$Revision: 655654 $>\nCopyright 1996 Adam Twiss, Zeus Technology Ltd, http://www.zeustech.net/\nLicensed to The Apache Software Foundation, http://www.apache.org/\n\nBenchmarking server (be patient)\napr_socket_recv: Connection reset by peer (104)\nTotal of 3 requests completed\n\nThis will lead to following output on the server side and leads into server using 100% CPU even after those subprocesses have exited:\n...\nSpawning\nSpawned\nSpawning\nError: Error: spawn EMFILE\nSpawning\nError: Error: spawn EMFILE\nSpawning\nError: Error: spawn EMFILE\nSpawning\nSpawned\n\nWe also can not create any new connections to the server, as the file handles and sockets remain open. I have following system constraints in use:\n$ node --version\nv0.10.5\n$ ulimit -a\ncore file size          (blocks, -c) 0\ndata seg size           (kbytes, -d) unlimited\nscheduling priority             (-e) 0\nfile size               (blocks, -f) unlimited\npending signals                 (-i) 256825\nmax locked memory       (kbytes, -l) 64\nmax memory size         (kbytes, -m) unlimited\nopen files                      (-n) 1024\npipe size            (512 bytes, -p) 8\nPOSIX message queues     (bytes, -q) 819200\nreal-time priority              (-r) 0\nstack size              (kbytes, -s) 8192\ncpu time               (seconds, -t) unlimited\nmax user processes              (-u) 256825\nvirtual memory          (kbytes, -v) unlimited\nfile locks                      (-x) unlimited\n\n\nThanks for the bug report. It's fixed in joyent/libuv@b3ab332.\n"}

{"6331631/a21dafb09821a4b5bd0d78171cbabd86fc06fd4d": "engines/rados: changed polling to completion callbacks methodology\n\nPreviously, getevent() function polled operations that have been submitted to check if some of them have already finished.\nNow, each rados operation invokes completion callback. This callback adds to list of completed operations.\nIn this version getevent() only peeks if completed list has some operations.\n\nThere are two benefits:\n1) small one - this works faster then previous version\n2) big one - when there is a huge amount of operations in fly, getevent() might be more overloaded then producer. In this scenario some old operations are finished, but not picked up and properly noticed. This caused absurdely long execution times, when actually everything was working properly.\n\nSigned-off-by: Adam Kupczyk <akupczyk@redhat.com>"}

{"6331631/a77e8ab428a842327af069083ee96cba73caf1d8": "fuzz: avoid building t/fuzz/parse_ini by default\n\nWith a vanilla build t/fuzz/parse_ini will segfault because the symbol\nFUZZING_BUILD_MODE_UNSAFE_FOR_PRODUCTION is not defined. If the symbol\nFUZZING_BUILD_MODE_UNSAFE_FOR_PRODUCTION is defined, fio won't tear down\nshared memory on program termination. Not tearing down shared memory is\nnecessary for t/fuzz/parse_ini to work correctly.\n\nDon't build t/fuzz/parse_ini unless CFLAGS contains\n-DFUZZING_BUILD_MODE_UNSAFE_FOR_PRODUCTION.\n\nSigned-off-by: Vincent Fu <vincent.fu@samsung.com>"}

{"44137852/077bd665bdd881c33b3086541365e36b4c733d57": "mailto: Wait for server opening message\n\nA subset of all mailserver configurations reject SMTP sessions where the\nclient sends anything before the server sent its opening message. This\nis the case with Exim on Ubuntu Artful, for example:\n\n  SMTP protocol synchronization error (input sent without waiting for\n  greeting)\n\nNot waiting for the opening message is a violation of the session\ninitiation described in RFC5321 (section 3.1). Update the mailto\nnotifier to wait for a non-error status code from the server before\nproceeding to start the mail transaction."}

https://github.com/galaxyproject/tools-iuc/blob/3e955a4c015cdb4df68fb530ace165be9877f94a/tools/bedtools/test-data/srma_in3.bam?raw=true\nI guess this is all linked ?\n\nBAM files contain two representations of the headers: the same text as in a SAM file, and a binary table of the @SQ chromosome names and lengths. Your srma_in3.bam file has a binary table listing dummy_chr and no text headers at all. (It is uncommon but valid to have binary sequence tables entries but no corresponding text @SQ headers in a BAM file.)\nsamtools view outputs SAM by synthesising @SQ text for any binary table entries if @SQ text is otherwise missing. So samtools view srma_in3.bam displays @SQ SN:dummy_chr \u2026 even though the input file doesn't really contain that in textual form.\nbedtools tag is outputting the headers by just passing the text form of the input headers directly, which is empty, which leads to the truncated file error you saw. Instead, it needs to do the same thing as samtools view: output the headers by calling sam_hdr_write on the input bam_hdr_t* rather than via an abbreviated text form of the headers.\nThe bamtofastq segfault is a separate problem and is a simple typo:\nindex 30c0a968..1f88a351 100644\n--- a/src/bamToFastq/bamToFastq.cpp\n+++ b/src/bamToFastq/bamToFastq.cpp\n@@ -38,8 +38,8 @@ BamToFastq::~BamToFastq(void) {}\n void BamToFastq::SingleFastq() {\n     // open the 1st fastq file for writing\n     _fq = new ofstream(_fastq1.c_str(), ios::out);\n-    if ( !*_fq1 ) {\n-        cerr << \"Error: The first fastq file (\" << _fastq1 << \") could not be opened. {"6367886/25155c00f80d81f7e742df95950eafca18ef8b02": "mmal: Add KEEP_PORT_FORMATS flag to mmal connection\n\nThere's a slightly quirky use case with deinterlacing.\nInterlaced YUV420 is line interleaved, so chroma line 1\nis that for luma lines 1&3, and chroma line 2 is for luma lines 2&4.\nIf you pass such a frame into a standard component (eg the ISP), it\nmesses up the chroma by assuming chroma line 1 is for luma lines 1&2.\n\nIf you pass it into the ISP as double width by half height then the\nchroma subsampling behaves correctly. Normally that precludes using\na mmal_connection as that will copy the port format from output\nto input. Setting this new flag skips that stage and makes it the\nclient's responsibilty to set both port formats appropriately."}

Take a look at nc, or iperf, or other tools that can listen or connect to a remote listener.  Essentially all of them use a switch to determine whether to run as a server or client, because it makes things clearer, and it's a whole lot safer to parse a switch than to parse a free-form argument.\n\nOut of interest, what does this change actually do over and above the tcp\noption?\n\u2026\nOn 5 December 2016 at 12:48, Austin S. The code appears to read it and populate serv_addr.sin_addr.s_addr with it.\n\nOkay, how about:\nraspivid -l tcp://0.0.0.0:1234 for listening for connection\nraspivid -o tcp://192.168.1.2:1234 for sending\nIs there much value in supporting both writing to a file and sending over a network (I'm thinking not much, but others may have different ideas).\n\n\nOkay, how about:\nraspivid -l tcp://0.0.0.0:1234 for listening for connection\nraspivid -o tcp://192.168.1.2:1234 for sending\n\nI'm good with that.\n\nIs there much value in supporting both writing to a file and sending over a network (I'm thinking not much, but others may have different ideas).\n\nSimultaneously? That's why I thought, if I change so much, it could not be a big deal to use a regular file write and network write simultaneously. Some people can find such feature useful, e.g. live observation over network and simultaneously write to a file on a PI.\nSome examples one more time:\n#wait for an incoming tcp connection on all interfaces.\n#After a client is connected, start sending data to a client\n#and write it to a local file simultaneously\nraspivid -o myfile.h264 -on tcp://0.0.0.0:1234 -l\n#wait for an incoming tcp connection on network interface\n#which has a given IP 192.168.1.2 assigned\n#after a client is connected, start sending data to a client\nraspivid -on tcp://192.168.1.2:1234 -l\n#wait for an incoming udp packet on all interfaces\n#after a client is connected, start sending data to a client\nraspivid -on udp://0.0.0.0:1234 -l\n#start sending UDP packets to a remote IPv4 host 195.12.1.2:1234\n#and save the video stream to a local file myfile simultaneously\nraspivid -on udp://195.12.1.2:1234 -o myfile\n#initiate a TCP connection to a remote IPv4 host 195.12.1.2:1234\n#and save video to a file simultaneously\nraspivid -on tcp://195.12.1.2:1234 -o myfile\n#initiate a TCP connection to a remote IPv4 host 195.12.1.2:1234\n#and send video to it\nraspivid -on tcp://195.12.1.2:1234\nWhat do you think?\n\nNo interest in network listening features?\n\nI have reservations about adding more big new features to Raspivid/still. They were meant as demo code and relatively simple examples of how to communicate with the camera. They're morphing into huge things which have so many bells and whistles that trying to grasp the basics is becoming harder.\nWhile network listening is just modifying the setup that is relatively simple to comprehend. What are you considering as a UDP connect? Such a thing does not exist as UDP is a connection-less protocol.\nThe client could send a UDP packet to the server, the server can extract the remote IP address and port, and then start sending data back to that address, but it's not a connect.\n\nSorry,\n\"UDP connect\" is poorly expressed. UDP is a connection-less protocol. But sometimes it does matter which of counterparts sends the first packet, for a communication to be possible.\nImagine following:\na RPI is connected to your home Router with a real IPv4 address. You have set up a UDP port forwarding rule to you RPI, so that your RPI is accesible from Internet by UDP. You want to stream a video from a RPI to your smartphone. A smartphone is also behind a NAT, but without port forwarding(typical LTE/3G connection). In order to communicate with each other, a first UDP packet must come from a smartphone to a RPI, so that a NAT table of your LTE/3G mobile provider got initialized and the RPI->smartphone way back UDP packets would not be dropped in a NAT router of your 3G mobile provider.\nThat is what I meant with:\n#wait for an incoming udp packet on all interfaces\n#after a client is connected, start sending data to a client\nraspivid -on udp://0.0.0.0:1234 -l\nBut OK, if you want to keep raspivid simple, I would just add a new -l switch for TCP listening only and add some more error handling (-EINTR).\nShould I also fix an already pulled code that lacks an error handling if no port given?\nTry to run with a camera attached :)\nraspivid -o tcp://192.168.0.2\n\nI know the use case you're meaning - effectively you're auto-configuring the remote IP address/port based on whoever last sent data to your port. It does require a chunk more changes that make the code harder to understand. There are things happening in the new year that should allow resourcing of tidying stuff up, so at that point adding UDP \"listen\" in a tidy manner is feasible.\nError handling on your patch would be good.\nIf there's a way to crash the existing code, then a second patch (can be on the same PR) to fix that happily accepted.\n\nCan you squash these two commits now?\nIf one had implemented just TCP listening, and the other just fixing the seg fault on missing port, then having them as two commits makes sense. {"6367886/87ccdff60a2ba43dc2e44bb7b28877726eda0d17": "Raspi[Still|Vid]Yuv: Add option for just saving luma\n\nFixes #170\n\nOption for saving only the luma / Y plane of the image data.\nIs it possible to output Y values only and discarding U and V values?\n\nWell, the good part about the I420 image format that it outputs is that the Y values come first, and then U, and then V, so truncating the file to a size of width*height bytes will do it without messing around with the source.\nHowever it wouldn't be super hard to hack in an option to RaspiStillYUV.c -- inside camera_buffer_callback, you could check pData->pstate->(some option) and then just write something smaller than buffer->length.\n\n@chewep is this still an issue?\n\n@chewep is this still an issue?\n\nNot even necessary to hack raspiyuv for this - simply pipe the output through the standard head command to chop off the UV tail and you should be good to go. Just calculate the number of bytes you need (bearing in mind the camera rounding horizontal resolution up to 32, and the vertical up to 16).\nFor example, with a V1 camera module capturing at 2592x1944 the amount of data output by raspiyuv will be 2592 * 1952 * 1.5 (1952 because of the vertical rounding, 1.5 because YUV420 format includes Y values at full resolution and U and V values at quarter resolution each). We only want the first 2592 * 1952 = 5059584 bytes of information, which the following command line should achieve:\n$ raspiyuv -o - | head -c $((2592*1952)) > output.yuv\n\nJust adjust the -c value according to your resolution and you should be good to go.\n\nYou can use head, but that won't work for timelapse sensibly.\nBoredom, so just making up a PR. raspividyuv also appears to be missing support for RGB mode, so stuffing that in at the same time.\n\nPatches pushed to my tree, but totally untested beyond \"they build\", so need to do that before creating a PR.\n"}

{"2984156/7c5d6b78942c3eee17e14c04bb2cc3a294968c52": "f3read/f3write: avoid the execution stack to list files\n\nFor large number of files, the original, recursive version of\n__ls_my_files() triggers a stack overflow; see issue #153 for\nan example.\n\nThis patch replaces __ls_my_files() with a version that replaces\nthe recursion with two scans of the target folder.\nI'm trying it on drives with different sizes and it appears the default 1GB file size for each of the *.h2w file might be an issue. The capacity of drives are getting larger these days. A fixed file size may limit it's usage in the near future. Can we pass commands to set the file size? Or let it set by it self based on the size of the drives?\n\nWhat kind of problem are you predicting? How would you choose a size for the H2W files to address that problem?\nThere are file systems that don't support files larger than ~2GB, so this change potentially opens compatibility issues. In addition, some users have already reported that they have successfully used F3 to check RAID volumes of multiple terabytes.\n\n@AltraMayor I'm currently checking a raid volume and the file system is zfs. It has 10 drives and the total capacity is 90TB. I tested it by fio, and the sequential write speed can reach 900MB/s. Causing the checking process takes a bit long (90 TB) / (500 (MB / s)) is around 2 days.\nI'm not quite sure the speed issue is caused by the file size, but if it can be set, I'd set to 10GB/50GB/100GB to see if the speed issue can be solved.\nIf we let it auto set based on the size of the drives, for file systems that don't support files larger than ~2GB, could we have a fallback method to 1GB? Or is there any method to detect the file system before checking? Or if these methods are not easy, let's just go with manually set.\n\nI expect that larger files won't speed up as much as you're hoping (if at all). One thing that does hold f3write/f3readback in term of performance are the random numbers written into the files, but there's no way around it. The solution that people have used to increase throughput in tests like yours is to have multiple instances of f3write/f3read. In your case, two or three should be enough. Make sure to use parameters --start-at and --end-at to coordinate the work of the multiple instances.\n\n@AltraMayor Hi again. Thanks for the reply, I have finished f3write after a few days. fault\n\n\nThe easiest way to understand a segmentation fault is to run f3read using gdb. gdb would run f3read until the segmentation fault is triggered, and allow one to inspect where in the code it happened and inspect the variables. But if you are not familiar with gdb, it can be confusing.\nThe first step is to compile F3 with debug information and without code optimization, which already is the default in the Makefile; unless you are passing parameters to the Makefile. The second step is to run f3read in the folder of the source, so  gdb can locate the source files. You would use the command sudo gdb ./f3read and enter the GDB command run  -s 1 -e 2 /tank/.\nOnce the execution stops due to the segmentation fault, copy the location information where the code stopped and the output of the GDB command bt. I'll likely need more than that, but I don't know what to ask without that output.\nIf you are familiar with gdb, you can likely figure out what triggered the segmentation fault by inspecting variables and using breakpoints. If you are not familiar with gdb but would like to give it a shot, you can find some short videos listed here.\n\nRunning a back of the envelope calculation, I arrived at the possibility that the large number of files is leading to a stack overflow. If this is the case, you can compile F3 with a larger stack to fix it:\nexport LDFLAGS=\"$LDFLAGS --stack,32000000\"\nmake\n\n32MB is likely too big, but it skips testing the needed size.\n\nThank you for the reply. One thing that does hold f3write/f3readback in term of performance are the random numbers written into the files, but there's no way around it. The solution that people have used to increase throughput in tests like yours is to have multiple instances of f3write/f3read. In your case, two or three should be enough. Make sure to use parameters --start-at and --end-at to coordinate the work of the multiple instances.\n\n@AltraMayor Hi again. Thanks for the reply, I have finished f3write after a few days. happened and inspect the variables. But if you are not familiar with gdb, it can be confusing.\nThe first step is to compile F3 with debug information and without code optimization, which already is the default in the Makefile; unless you are passing parameters to the Makefile. The second step is to run f3read in the folder of the source, so  gdb can locate the source files. You would use the command sudo gdb ./f3read and enter the GDB command run  -s 1 -e 2 /tank/.\nOnce the execution stops due to the segmentation fault, copy the location information where the code stopped and the output of the GDB command bt. {"2316310/40e66e2e8be046f909dfff73b3909f15c9d0f400": "Update release notes, etc., for the 1.44.4 release\n\nSigned-off-by: Theodore Ts'o <tytso@mit.edu>"}
{"2316310/4b3038134baf81c6f9bd36dbbf565ea66e46331f": "resize2fs: update checksums in the extent tree's relocated block\n\nWhen shrinking an file system, and we need to relocate an inode, the\nchecksums in its extent tree must get updated to reflect its new inode\nnumber.  When doing this, we need to do this *after* we update the\nextent tree to reflect any blocks which need to be relocated due to\nthe file system shrink operation.\n\nOtherwise, in the case where only an interior node of the extent tree\nneeds to get relocated, and none of the entries in that node need to\nbe adjusted, the checksum for that interior node is updated in the old\ncopy of that block, and then after the extent tree is updated to use\nthe new copy of that interior node, the extent tree is left with an\ninvalid checksum.\n\nThis is a relatively rare case, since it requires the following\nconditions to be true:\n\n*)  The metadata checksum feature must be enabled.\n*)  An inode needs to be relocated.\n*)  The inode needs to have an interior node.\n*)  The block for that interior node needs to be relocated.\n*)  None of blocks addressed by entries in that interior node needs\n    to be relocated.\n\nWhen all of these conditions are true, though, the file system is left\nwith corrupted with bad checksum for the extent tree block.\n\nAddresses-Launchpad-Bug: 1798562\n\nSigned-off-by: Theodore Ts'o <tytso@mit.edu>\nReported-by: Jean-Baptiste Lallement <jean-baptiste.lallement@ubuntu.com>"}
{"2316310/ce75cf5459ee130dfe8980a6a16af1947d410ff1": "e2fsck: use \"Optimize?\" prompt for PR_1E_CAN_{COLLAPSE,NARROW}_EXTENT_TREE\n\nIf e2fsck can optimize an inode's extent tree, it should ask for\npermission to optimize it, not to \"fix\" it.  This was causing some\nconfusion, since some users interpreted this prompt as an indication\nthat the file system was inconsistent.\n\nSigned-off-by: Theodore Ts'o <tytso@mit.edu>"}
{"2316310/f6c50d68cc044422f4f38b7c8be892527d66e10d": "mke2fs: fix bugs in hugefile creation\n\nFor certain sizes mke2fs's hugefile creation would fail with the error:\n\nmke2fs: Could not allocate block in ext2 filesystem while creating huge file 0\n\nThis would happen because we had failed to reserve enough space for\nthe metadata blocks for the hugefile.  There were two problems:\n\n1) The overhead calculation function failed to take into account the\ncluster size for bigalloc file systems.\n\n2) In the case where num_blocks is 0 and num_files is 1, the overhead\ncalculation function was passed a size of 0, which caused the\ncalculated overhead to be zero.\n\nGoogle-Bug-Id: 123239032\nSigned-off-by: Theodore Ts'o <tytso@mit.edu>"}

Last update 24f2cb3...59b0c42. Read the comment docs.\n\n\nRedisRaft works only on Redis 6.0 and above.- with your current changes changing raft configuration on the fly is supported only for Redis 7.0 - do we want to update the doc too?\n\nUpdated PR, not using module args anymore.\n\nI think Module developers will all love this feature, thanks.\n\n@redis/core-team Please take a look at the top comment and provide feedback about the implementation. There are still a couple of checks failing in the CI, so once those are cleaned up and we have consensus on the finalized implementation I'll ping the group again.\n\nCore group meeting decisions:\n\nWe should add a new API to register an apply callback that is executed after all of values have been set during config set. This allows simple modules to define just set callbacks, but more advanced used cases should be able to register this apply config. The apply callback should provide at least a list of config names.\nThe current Apply API is likely okay, it makes it straight forward to reason about the values.\nWe didn't fully align on default values. The major points are:\n\n\nWe would prefer defining default values once, perhaps during registration. This value is what is compared against when doing rewrites.\nWe aren't sure if configs should be able to force being included or excluded from a rewrite.\n\n\nfew notes:\ni'm not certain the new apply callback must get names of all configs that were changed (it's a complexity i think we can leave out, and let modules that care handle on their own.\ni see two options here: one is to have just one global apply callback per module, and the other is to have an optional apply callback per config (in which case redis can detect that several modified configs had the same apply callback, and call it only once, like we do for our internal configs).\nregarding defaults, the important requirements in my eyes are: first that we avoid making something explicit in rewrite so that we can later change default and still distinguish between explicit changes and implicit ones.\nand secondly that if we do let the module provide a default value, we must avoid a case where the real default (for startup) could be different than the one used for rewrite, so i think it must be at registration time, and then the module will always get a callback at startup, and can't distinguish between an implicit callback, and a case where the user actually provided a config (i think this is acceptable)\njust to mention that we agree that enums need better handling, not just an array of valid strings.\n\n@madolson @oranagra one thing that has come up with our work on RedisRaft is that we want to base some RedisRaft configuration based on redis configuration (tls configuration in this case). It could be nice if one could get the callback as well on general redis \"config set\" commands.\nthoughts?\n\n@sjpotter i don't think this should be part of the configuration infrastructure. i think that maybe similarly to RM_GetServerInfo and RM_GetCommandKeysWithFlags, we should add some API to access configuration (i.e. as an alternative to getting it with RM_Call(\"config\").\nAnd we could add add an event / hook like RedisModuleEvent_ReplicationRoleChanged, notifying modules about configuration changes.\ni.e. not an event per config, but rather a generic RedisModuleEvent_ConfigChanged that carries the config name.\n\nFew updates here. ARGS is no longer required as an argument to loadex. There's now also an optional apply callback, and a default value that can be specified during registration. Enums now work based off ints (I went with the implicit index implementation.) Renamed RM_ApplyConfigs to RM_LoadConfigs to avoid confusion with new apply callbacks. Also a few documentation updates, and removed '.' restriction in config names. module configs always have a '.', but standardConfigs in the future can as well.\n\nNote the forcepush was to clean up a bunch of small commits that made up the new features\n\n@redis/core-team please approve the API.\nNote the unresolved comment above (currently using char* as error).\n@nichchun please update the top comment about recent changes.\n\nI also prefer char*, using a heap allocated string is more burden for the common use case (static errors).\nif we stick with char* only the ones that need to format a string have more burden.\n@yossigo please look at the last commit and state if you agree to revert it.\n(if not, we should at least unify some common code)\n\n@oranagra As discussed, I don't feel strongly about it but do think the RedisModuleString option is uglier/more boiler plate but also more correct from an API perspective. Pushed a commit to reduce duplication and a small test fix.\n\nmerged \ud83c\udf89\nThank you @nichchun and everyone involved.\n\nCI failed on alpine.\nhttps://github.com/redis/redis/runs/5763628464?check_suite_focus=true\nhttps://github.com/redis/redis/runs/5763628410?check_suite_focus=true\n\n@nichchun ^\n"}

{"389238061/dba7b7e830218d814fbb24ba0c89dd74f76707af": "refactor: Major code cleanup\n\nMostly plumbing.\n\nSummary:\n\n - Key processing logic remains untouched.\n - Simplified IPC logic.\n - Streamlined the event loop.\n - Consolidated config and descriptor parsing logic into config.[ch].\n - Eliminated internal KEYD_* environment variables in favour of compile time macros.\n - Added color coding to the daemon output.\n - Load all configuration files on initialization.\n\nThe last change increases initialization time (on the order of \u03bcs) and resident\nmemory size, but simplifies the config logic and has the benefit of linting all\nfiles immediately while also allowing for the introduction of in-daemon\nreloading. This ultimately results in a net saving for the user since\nmost service managers are horrendously slow :(.\n\nIt also means hotplugged devices will use the in-memory config set (which\nprobably makes more sense anyway) instead of dynamically scanning the config\ndirectory. This is more efficient for small config directories (<100 files),\nbut less efficient for large ones (uncommon)."}

{"95347622/670949511b1de69320629a1d0c6f858684691cb5": "Rework max_udp_payload_size\n\nPreviously, ngtcp2_conn_writev_stream always fully utilizes the given\nbuffer.  This commit changes this behaviour and by default it limits\nthe UDP payload size to 1200 bytes as described in RFC9000.\n\nngtcp2_settings.no_udp_payload_size_shaping brings back the old\nbehaviour.\n\nIf ngtcp2_settings.assume_symmetric_path is set to nonzero and\nngtcp2_settings.no_udp_payload_size_shaping is set to zero, the\nmaximum UDP payload size is extended up to the maximum received UDP\npayload size.  The size is capped by\nngtcp2_settings.max_udp_payload_size.  This might be useful for server\nto enlarge UDP payload size depending on the client Initial packet\nsize."}

{"16143904/10c9e8f95254d8c6436c4d3cb093fa5544b45c90": "Cache hardware's arch_t id after querying once.\n\nDetails:\n- Added logic to bli_arch.c that will call what was previously the body\n  of bli_arch_query_id() only once and then cache the value in a static\n  variable local to the file. (Previously, the arch_t associated with\n  the hardware/configuration was queried every time bli_arch_query_id()\n  was called, which was at least once per level-3 function call. Thanks\n  to Devin Matthews for suggesting this feature via issue #175.\n- Added -lpthread to the compile/link command line of the compiler\n  invocation that compiles build/detect/config/config_detect.c, which\n  prints the string identifying the detected configuration, since it\n  is now needed due to new pthread_once() logic in bli_arch.c.\n- Implementation note: I chose to implement this arch_t caching feature\n  via pthread_once(), using a separate pthread_once_t variable local to\n  the file, rather than calling bli_init_once(). The reason is that I\n  did not want to require bli_init() as a prerequisite to this function.\n  bli_init() already calls several sub-components, some of which make use\n  of bli_arch_query_id(), and therefore it would be easy to fall into a\n  circular self-init situation (which usually causes pthreads to hang\n  indefinitely).\nIIRC the runtime config selection is supposed to just run once and be \"remembered\", but in debugging arm32 runtime selection with printfs, I get:\n% \n% level-3 implementations        s       d       c       z\nbli_cpuid_query_id\nbli_cpuid_query\nn1, n2, n3 = 42 20 103\nproc_str: model name  : ARMv7 Processor rev 4 (v7l)\nptno_str: CPU part    : 0xd03\nfeat_str: Features    : half thumb fastmult vfp edsp neon vfpv3 tls vfpv4 idiva idivt vfpd32 lpae evtstrm crc32 \nfeatures var: 1\nmodel: 0\npart#: d03\nbli_cpuid_query_id\nbli_cpuid_query\nn1, n2, n3 = 42 20 103\nproc_str: model name  : ARMv7 Processor rev 4 (v7l)\nptno_str: CPU part    : 0xd03\nfeat_str: Features    : half thumb fastmult vfp edsp neon vfpv3 tls vfpv4 idiva idivt vfpd32 lpae evtstrm crc32 \nfeatures var: 1\nmodel: 0\npart#: d03\nbli_cpuid_query_id\nbli_cpuid_query\nn1, n2, n3 = 42 20 103\nproc_str: model name  : ARMv7 Processor rev 4 (v7l)\nptno_str: CPU part    : 0xd03\nfeat_str: Features    : half thumb fastmult vfp edsp neon vfpv3 tls vfpv4 idiva idivt vfpd32 lpae evtstrm crc32 \nfeatures var: 1\nmodel: 0\npart#: d03\nbli_cpuid_query_id\nbli_cpuid_query\nn1, n2, n3 = 42 20 103\nproc_str: model name  : ARMv7 Processor rev 4 (v7l)\nptno_str: CPU part    : 0xd03\nfeat_str: Features    : half thumb fastmult vfp edsp neon vfpv3 tls vfpv4 idiva idivt vfpd32 lpae evtstrm crc32 \nfeatures var: 1\nmodel: 0\npart#: d03\n\n\nGlad to see my leaving those printf() calls in there was useful. :)\nIdeally, yes, the value would be cached somewhere. (The current design calls CPUID or equivalent each time bli_cpuid_query_id() is called. But this only happens for multi-configuration builds. If you configure via configure haswell or configure auto, only one configuration is selected and that selection is hard-coded into the definition of bli_arch_query_id().)\n\nI hadn't noticed this before.I don't know how much effect caching would have, but there's a reason to make doing it configurable -- checkpoint/restart in the common case of a heterogeneous cluster.\n\nYou mean like VM migration between incompatible hardware? I think that\u2019s a\nrather exceptional corner case. Checkpoint-restart in HPC means restarting\nthe program and reading in state. That will initialize shared libraries\nfrom scratch and doesn\u2019t break with caching the config.\n\nNo, I mean like using BLCR or, these days, probably DMTCP [unless you use PSM :-(].  It's a known problem with things like openblas that you need to restart on a compatible micro-architecture, though I'd be happy to know how to avoid that if the scheduler doesn't support it directly -- never added to SGE, for instance. I thought dumping the memory of HPC applications was what things like SCR are for.\nNot a big deal, anyhow.\n\n@loveshack I'm afraid I'm unfamiliar with any of the acronyms you mention above. Could you elaborate?\nI also was unaware of the need to \"restart\" OpenBLAS in certain situations. Could you say a few words about this as well?\n\n@fgvanzee Did you google these first?  In any case:\n\nBLCR is Berkeley Labs Checkpoint Restart.\nDMTCP\nPSM and PSM2 are Performance Scaled Messaging, which are the low-level APIs for Intel True Scale and Omni Path interconnects.\nSGE is Sun Grid Engine.\nSCR is Scalable Checkpoint Restart from LLNL.\n\n\n@jeffhammond I made no attempt to google any of them under concern for acronym collisions. :)\nThe links were marginally helpful, but I still don't see the application to the topic at hand. I don't understand why this isn't an issue of simply storing the result of a \"deep\" hardware query and using it to define future \"shallow\" queries.\n\nSorry for causing confusion talking to Jeff, but as I said it's not a big deal, and definitely not worth supporting if there's significant overhead, but:\nThis is about dumping the entire state of a running program to be able to restart it from that point after an error or running out of time for the job (as opposed to the application writing state itself and picking that up if re-run from scratch; Jeff will know of chemistry-ish programs that can do that, others that can't, and some that can only in some of their modes). Clearly if you dump the executing code that's made a dynamic SIMD selection, say avx512, and try to restart it on a system that's only, say, avx2, it fails. That may not happen on the sort of systems Jeff's thinking of, either because they're homogenous or require selecting a specific node type; however it can on one I've been used to using and managing. BLCR and DMTCP are just two systems for doing the dump-running-state checkpointing (entirely in user space with DMTCP). If you do a cpuid-type test on each call, it would solve the problem unless the checkpoint happened in the middle of such a call, which it probably wouldn't for an MPI application, for instance.\nSCR is a fancy user space filesystem for supporting such checkpoint data at large scale. OpenBLAS is just an example of an existing dynamic SIMD-selection library that can cause the sort of problem I'm talking about, like MKL, fftw, and some others -- nothing directly related to it.\n\n@loveshack Thanks for that explanation. I now understand how we got from CPUID selection to checkpointing. (It was not at all clear, even if it should have been. Sorry.)\nAnyhow, my response to this is that people who do this kind of checkpointing will simply need to make sure they configure BLIS in the appropriate way--that is, that they enable the more frequent CPUID method, rather than once-and-cache method. My read of the situation is that this would be sufficient for their purposes. Please correct me if I'm mistaken.\n\nTalking about algorithmic fault tolerance.  There is this delightful paper https://experts.illinois.edu/en/publications/fault-tolerant-high-performance-matrix-multiplication-theory-and- <https://experts.illinois.edu/en/publications/fault-tolerant-high-performance-matrix-multiplication-theory-and->\nthat was revisited with BLIS a few years ago, and then promptly rejected by IPDPS (or was it SCXX)?\n\u2026\n On Apr 19, 2018, at 3:56 PM, Field G. Van Zee ***@***.***> wrote:\n\n @loveshack <https://github.com/loveshack> Thanks for that explanation. I now understand how we got from CPUID selection to checkpointing. (It was not at all clear, even if it should have been. Sorry.)\n\n Anyhow, my response to this is that people who do this kind of checkpointing will simply need to make sure they configure BLIS in the appropriate way--that is, that they enable the more frequent CPUID method, rather than once-and-cache method. My read of the situation is that this would be sufficient for their purposes. {"16143904/3e116f0a2953f50b3c068759a775ad7ffae04e49": "Fixed imaginary bug in quadratic partitioning code.\n\nDetails:\n- Fixed a bug in the relatively new quadratic partitioning code that,\n  under the right conditions, would perform sqrt() on a negative value.\n  If the solution is imaginary, we discard it and use an alternate\n  partition width that assumes no diagonal intersection. That alternate\n  width is actually already computed, so, the fix was quite simple.\n  Thanks to Devangi Parikh for reporting this bug."}
{"16143904/701b9aa3ff028decbf90efac0dca5bd64fe26269": "Redesigned control tree infrastructure.\n\nDetails:\n- Altered control tree node struct definitions so that all nodes have the\n  same struct definition, whose primary fields consist of a blocksize id,\n  a variant function pointer, a pointer to an optional parameter struct,\n  and a pointer to a (single) sub-node. This unified control tree type is\n  now named cntl_t.\n- Changed the way control tree nodes are connected, and what computation\n  they represent, such that, for example, packing operations are now\n  associated with nodes that are \"inline\" in the tree, rather than off-\n  shoot braches. The original tree for the classic Goto gemm algorithm was\n  expressed (roughly) as:\n\n    blk_var2 -> blk_var3 -> blk_var1 -> ker_var2\n                         |           |\n                         -> packb    -> packa\n\n  and now, the same tree would look like:\n\n    blk_var2 -> blk_var3 -> packb -> blk_var1 -> packa -> ker_var2\n\n  Specifically, the packb and packa nodes perform their respective packing\n  operations and then recurse (without any loop) to a subproblem. This means\n  there are now two kinds of level-3 control tree nodes: partitioning and\n  non-partitioning. The blocked variants are members of the former, because\n  they iteratively partition off submatrices and perform suboperations on\n  those partitions, while the packing variants belong to the latter group.\n  (This change has the effect of allowing greatly simplified initialization\n  of the nodes, which previously involved setting many unused node fields to\n  NULL.)\n- Changed the way thrinfo_t tree nodes are arranged to mirror the new\n  connective structure of control trees. That is, packm nodes are no longer\n  off-shoot branches of the main algorithmic nodes, but rather connected\n  \"inline\".\n- Simplified control tree creation functions. Partitioning nodes are created\n  concisely with just a few fields needing initialization. By contrast, the\n  packing nodes require additional parameters, which are stored in a\n  packm-specific struct that is tracked via the optional parameters pointer\n  within the control tree struct. (This parameter struct must always begin\n  with a uint64_t that contains the byte size of the struct. This allows\n  us to use a generic function to recursively copy control trees.) gemm,\n  herk, and trmm control tree creation continues to be consolidated into\n  a single function, with the operation family being used to select\n  among the parameter-agnostic macro-kernel wrappers. A single routine,\n  bli_cntl_free(), is provided to free control trees recursively, whereby\n  the chief thread within a groups release the blocks associated with\n  mem_t entries back to the memory broker from which they were acquired.\n- Updated internal back-ends, e.g. bli_gemm_int(), to query and call the\n  function pointer stored in the current control tree node (rather than\n  index into a local function pointer array). Before being invoked, these\n  function pointers are first cast to a gemm_voft (for gemm, herk, or trmm\n  families) or trsm_voft (for trsm family) type, which is defined in\n  frame/3/bli_l3_var_oft.h.\n- Retired herk and trmm internal back-ends, since all execution now flows\n  through gemm or trsm blocked variants.\n- Merged forwards- and backwards-moving variants by querying the direction\n  from routines as a function of the variant's matrix operands. gemm and\n  herk always move forward, while trmm and trsm move in a direction that\n  is dependent on which operand (a or b) is triangular.\n- Added functions bli_thread_get_range_mdim(), bli_thread_get_range_ndim(),\n  each of which takes additional arguments and hides complexity in managing\n  the difference between the way ranges are computed for the four families\n  of operations.\n- Simplified level-3 blocked variants according to the above changes, so that\n  the only steps taken are:\n  1. Query partitioning direction (forwards or backwards).\n  2. Prune unreferenced regions, if they exist.\n  3. Determine the thread partitioning sub-ranges.\n  <begin loop>\n    4. Determine the partitioning blocksize (passing in the partitioning\n       direction)\n    5. Acquire the curren iteration's partitions for the matrices affected\n       by the current variants's partitioning dimension (m, k, n).\n    6. Call the subproblem.\n  <end loop>\n- Instantiate control trees once per thread, per operation invocation.\n  (This is a change from the previous regime in which control trees were\n  treated as stateless objects, initialized with the library, and shared\n  as read-only objects between threads.) This once-per-thread allocation\n  is done primarily to allow threads to use the control tree as as place\n  to cache certain data for use in subsequent loop iterations. Presently,\n  the only application of this caching is a mem_t entry for the packing\n  blocks checked out from the memory broker (allocator). If a non-NULL\n  control tree is passed in by the (expert) user, then the tree is copied\n  by each thread. This is done in bli_l3_thread_decorator(), in\n  bli_thrcomm_*.c.\n- Added a new field to the context, and opid_t which tracks the \"family\"\n  of the operation being executed. For example, gemm, hemm, and symm are\n  all part of the gemm family, while herk, syrk, her2k, and syr2k are\n  all part of the herk family. Knowing the operation's family is necessary\n  when conditionally executing the internal (beta) scalar reset on on\n  C in blocked variant 3, which is needed for gemm and herk families,\n  but must not be performed for the trmm family (because beta has only\n  been applied to the current row-panel of C after the first rank-kc\n  iteration).\n- Reexpressed 3m3 induced method blocked variant in frame/3/gemm/ind\n  to comform with the new control tree design, and renamed the macro-\n  kernel codes corresponding to 3m2 and 4m1b.\n- Renamed bli_mem.c (and its APIs) to bli_memsys.c, and renamed/relocated\n  bli_mem_macro_defs.h from frame/include to frame/base/bli_mem.h.\n- Renamed/relocated bli_auxinfo_macro_defs.h from frame/include to\n  frame/base/bli_auxinfo.h.\n- Fixed a minor bug whereby the storage-to-ukr-preference matching\n  optimization in the various level-3 front-ends was not being applied\n  properly when the context indicated that execution would be via an\n  induced method. (Before, we always checked the native micro-kernel\n  corresponding to the datatype being executed, whereas now we check\n  the native micro-kernel corresponding to the datatype's real projection,\n  since that is the micro-kernel that is actually used by induced methods.\n- Added an option to the testsuite to skip the testing of native level-3\n  complex implementations. Previously, it was always tested, provided that\n  the c/z datatypes were enabled. However, some configurations use\n  reference micro-kernels for complex datatypes, and testing these\n  implementations can slow down the testsuite considerably."}
{"16143904/375eb30b0a63ac06a363a5f75f283584258db48b": "Added mixed-precision support to 1m method.\n\nDetails:\n- Lifted the constraint that 1m only be used when all operands' storage\n  datatypes (along with the computation datatype) are equal. Now, 1m may\n  be used as long as all operands are stored in the complex domain. This\n  change largely consisted of adding the ability to pack to 1e and 1r\n  formats from one precision to another. It also required adding logic\n  for handling complex values of alpha to bli_packm_blk_var1_md()\n  (similar to the logic in bli_packm_blk_var1()).\n- Fixed a bug in several virtual microkernels (bli_gemm_md_c2r_ref.c,\n  bli_gemm1m_ref.c, and bli_gemmtrsm1m_ref.c) that resulted in the wrong\n  ukernel output preference field being read. Previously, the preference\n  for the native complex ukernel was being read instead of the pref for\n  the native real domain ukernel. This bug would not manifest if the\n  preference for the native complex ukernel happened to be equal to that\n  of the native real ukernel.\n- Added support for testing mixed-precision 1m execution via the gemm\n  module of the testsuite.\n- Tweaked/simplified bli_gemm_front() and bli_gemm_md.c so that pack\n  schemas are always read from the context, rather than trying to\n  sometimes embed them directly to the A and B objects. (They are still\n  embedded, but now uniformly only after reading the schemas from the\n  context.)\n- Redefined cpp macro bli_l3_ind_recast_1m_params() as a static function\n  and renamed to bli_gemm_ind_recast_1m_params() (since gemm is the only\n  consumer).\n- Added 1m optimization logic (via bli_gemm_ind_recast_1m_params()) to\n  bli_gemm_ker_var2_md().\n- Added explicit handling for beta == 1 and beta == 0 in the reference\n  gemm1m virtual microkernel in ref_kernels/ind/bli_gemm1m_ref.c.\n- Rewrote various level-0 macro defs, including axpyris, axpbyris,\n  scal2ris, and xpbyris (and their conjugating counterparts) to\n  explicitly support three operand types and updated invocations to\n  xpbyris in bli_gemmtrsm1m_ref.c.\n- Query and use the storage datatype of the packed object instead of the\n  storage datatype of the source object in bli_packm_blk_var1().\n- Relocated and renamed frame/ind/misc/bli_l3_ind_opt.h to\n  frame/3/gemm/ind/bli_gemm_ind_opt.h.\n- Various whitespace/comment updates."}

{"89948865/d13b72b3887096f54d3570b723f2f518cf0e903b": "FIX : Socket leakage on error #6\n\nCleanup open file descriptors when bind_sockaddr fails.\n\nThanks to @hyperblast for reporting this issue\n\nhttps://github.com/criticalstack/libevhtp/issues/6\nevhtp_bind_sockaddr() does not close created socket if bind() fails:\nhttps://github.com/criticalstack/libevhtp/blob/develop/evhtp.c#L3668\nif evhtp_accept_socket() fails for some reason, socked is leaked as well:\nhttps://github.com/criticalstack/libevhtp/blob/develop/evhtp.c#L3673\n\nThanks for the heads up, looking now.\n\nHi again @hyperblast - I just pushed a quick patch here d13b72b\nSeems to fix it; any suggestions?\n\nHi @NathanFrench, thanks for the fix.\nI think it resolves the first issue, but second one still remains.\nevhtp_accept_socket() gets called as the last statement of evhtp_bind_sockaddr().\nNormally evhtp_accept_socket() gets ownership of fd because the evconnlistener_new() is called with LEV_OPT_CLOSE_ON_FREE option.\nAssuming I understood libevent sources right  evconnlistener_new() does not close socket when error occurs:\nhttps://github.com/libevent/libevent/blob/a73fb2f443ebf9687ee6ca81a6401d1f3751683f/listener.c#L155\nsetsockopt() calls in the evhtp_accept_socket() might fail as well.\nSo to sum up, I think that evhtp_bind_sockaddr() should check result of call to evhtp_accept_socket() and close the socket on error to prevent leakage.\nP.S. Thanks for having this project up and running. Keep it up!\n\nI'm not too familiar with Libevent's listener backend, but I think you're right. 18f7ec0 has been updated.\n\nHi Nathan,\nI think the fix has possibly unintended side effect of changing evhtp_bind_sockaddr() logic to close socket on error.\nIt's a public function that could be called by library users directly (e.g. not via evhtp_bind_socket().\nNew behavior might be a good thing, but it's worth noting that this is a breaking change.\n\nWe already make a determination on the ownership of a /connection/, which must be relinquished if the take_ownership function is called. But we don't do the same for file descriptors.\nA user can pass their own file descriptor in, and we claim ownership, should we add a flag that specifically designates the owner of the file descriptor? Or maybe a better tree of perms:\nbase [api owned] -> server [api owned] -> connection [user/api owned] -> request -> [user/api owned]\n\nOk, so this change technically should not break users if they used the function documentation :D. We weren't conforming to the documentation.\nd0347dc should address most of these issues including an extra note.\n\n\nOk, so this change technically should not break users if they used the function documentation\n\nIt seems I've referenced wrong function in my previous comment, but you've already figured out what I've actually meant. Thank you.\n\nA user can pass their own file descriptor in, and we claim ownership, should we add a flag that specifically designates the owner of the file descriptor?\n\nHaving externally owned socket might have a use-case. For example some kind of sort-restart. User wants to keep the listening socket, but recreate evhtp instance. However evhtp_accept_socket() calls listen() (via evconnlistener_new()) and setsockopt(). This might prevent socket reuse.\nSo to make this scenario work there should be a version of evconnlistener_new() that expects socket that is already in a listening state. AFAIK, there is no such function at the moment.\n\nI merged the changes with the proper checking and logic.
